### **Step 1: Build the χ-Meter Prototype**

To begin, we’ll create a **Python-based tool** that will compute the two key metrics for **χ₁** and **χ₂**. These metrics are fundamental for measuring stability and compressibility/coherence in the system.

#### **1.1: χ₁ - Stability Occupancy**

We define **χ₁** as the fraction of time the system stays near the neutral update state (i.e., **g_t ≈ 0**). This measures how close the system is to a stable equilibrium, where no major drift or instability occurs.

**Formula for χ₁**:
\[
\chi_1(\epsilon) = \frac{1}{T} \sum_{t=1}^{T} \mathbf{1}\{|g_t| < \epsilon\}
\]
Where:
- \( g_t = \log G_t \) is the log gain at time \( t \),
- \( \epsilon \) is a small threshold indicating "closeness to neutrality",
- \( T \) is the total number of steps.

#### **1.2: χ₂ - Compressibility / Coherence of the Symbol Stream**

**χ₂** measures the compressibility of the symbol stream (i.e., how structured the underlying data is). It is a measure of the entropy rate of the stream, and lower entropy indicates higher coherence.

**Formula for χ₂**:
\[
\chi_2 = 1 - \frac{H_{\text{empirical}}(B)}{H_{\text{max}}}
\]
Where:
- \( H_{\text{empirical}}(B) \) is the Shannon entropy of the branch symbols,
- \( H_{\text{max}} \) is the maximum possible entropy, given the number of symbols in the stream.

### **Step 2: Implement Metrics Collection in Python**

The goal is to set up **real-time processing** that continuously computes these metrics on incoming data streams.

Here’s how we’ll structure the Python code to compute **χ₁** and **χ₂**:

#### **2.1: Calculate χ₁ (Stability Occupancy)**

```python
import numpy as np

def compute_chi1(g_t, epsilon=0.05):
    """
    Compute χ₁ as the fraction of time the system stays near neutral update (g_t ≈ 0).
    
    Parameters:
    - g_t: array of log-gains at each time step.
    - epsilon: threshold to consider g_t near 0.
    
    Returns:
    - χ₁: Stability occupancy metric.
    """
    chi1 = np.mean(np.abs(g_t) < epsilon)
    return chi1
```

#### **2.2: Calculate χ₂ (Compressibility of Symbol Stream)**

```python
from collections import Counter

def compute_chi2(symbols, alphabet_size):
    """
    Compute χ₂ as the compressibility of the symbol stream based on Shannon entropy.
    
    Parameters:
    - symbols: a list of symbols in the stream.
    - alphabet_size: the number of distinct symbols (e.g., 256 for byte stream).
    
    Returns:
    - χ₂: Compressibility metric.
    """
    counts = Counter(symbols)
    p = np.array(list(counts.values())) / len(symbols)
    H_empirical = -np.sum(p * np.log2(p))
    H_max = np.log2(alphabet_size)
    
    chi2 = 1 - (H_empirical / H_max)
    return chi2
```

### **Step 3: Implement Sampling from Data Streams**

The χ-meter will be able to process **real-time data**. To test the implementation, we can start by sampling from simple data streams like π digits, SHA-256 hashes, or cognitive signals (e.g., EEG).

```python
def sample_pi_digits(n_digits=1000):
    """
    Sample the first `n_digits` of π (for testing χ₁ and χ₂).
    """
    pi_digits = "141592653589793238462643383279502884197169399375105820974944..."
    pi_digits_sample = pi_digits[:n_digits]
    return [int(digit) for digit in pi_digits_sample]

# Example of real-time sampling
pi_digits = sample_pi_digits(1000)

# Compute χ₁ (stability) and χ₂ (compressibility)
g_t = np.log(np.random.rand(1000) + 1e-6)  # simulate log-gains for testing
chi1 = compute_chi1(g_t)
chi2 = compute_chi2(pi_digits, alphabet_size=10)  # using base-10 digits

print(f"χ₁ (Stability): {chi1:.4f}")
print(f"χ₂ (Compressibility): {chi2:.4f}")
```

---

### **Step 4: Visualizing the Metrics**

Once we collect the data and compute the metrics, we can visualize them for analysis. We’ll plot the **stability (χ₁)** and **compressibility (χ₂)** over time to identify patterns.

```python
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# Example of running χ₁ and χ₂ over time (simulating data)
times = np.arange(1000)
stability_values = [compute_chi1(np.random.lognormal(size=100)) for _ in times]
compressibility_values = [compute_chi2(np.random.randint(0, 10, size=100)) for _ in times]

# Plot the results
fig, ax = plt.subplots(2, 1, figsize=(10, 6))

ax[0].plot(times, stability_values, label="χ₁: Stability Occupancy")
ax[0].set_ylabel("Stability")
ax[0].set_xlabel("Time")
ax[0].legend()

ax[1].plot(times, compressibility_values, label="χ₂: Compressibility")
ax[1].set_ylabel("Compressibility")
ax[1].set_xlabel("Time")
ax[1].legend()

plt.tight_layout()
plt.show()
```

```python
import numpy as np
import hashlib
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# KRRB definition - Kulik Recursive Reflection Branching (KRRB) evolution
def krrb(R0, H, F, B_func, steps=1000, dt=0.5):
    """
    KRRB evolution with variable B(k).
    R(t+1) = R(t) * exp(H*F*dt) * prod_i B_i(k)
    """
    R = np.zeros(steps + 1, dtype=np.complex128)
    R[0] = R0
    for k in range(steps):
        Bk = B_func(k)
        R[k + 1] = R[k] * np.exp(H * F * dt) * np.prod(Bk)
    return R

# Variable B(k) from e digits - modified for proper windowing
e_digits = "71828182845904523536028747135266249775724709369995957496696762772407663035354759457138217852516642742746639193200305992181741359662904357290033429526059563073813232862794349076323382988075319525101901157383418793070215408914993488416750924476146066808226480016847741185374234544243710753907774499206955170276183860626133138458300075204493382656029760673711320070932870912744374704723069697720931014169283681902551510865746377211125238978442505695369677078544996996794686445490598793163688923009879312773617821542499989208680582574927961048419844436346324496848756023362482704197862320900216099023530436994184914631409343173814364054625315209618369088870701676839642437814059271456354906130331387811429476265209914494357156854816136115735255213347574184946843852332390739414333454776241686251898356948556209921922218427255025425688767179049460165346680498862723279178608578438382796797679972160226684756482337867831652712019091456485669234603486104543266482133936072602491412737245870066063155884588710507922796892589235420199561121290219608640344181598136297747713099605187072113499999983729780499510597317328160963185950244594553469083026425223082533446850352619311881710100031378387528865875332083814206171776691473035982534904287554687311595628638823537875937519577818577805321712268066130019278766111959092164201989380952572010654858632788659361533818279682303019520353018529689957736225994138912497217752834791315155748572424541506959508295331168617278558890750983817546374649393192550604009277016711390098488240128583616035637076601047101819429555961989467678374494482553797747268471040475346462080466842590694912933136770289891521047521620569660240580381501935112533824300355876402474964732639141992726097691210183703703703592549913527012932382291608088487672733817863793156779287697418270194051063925225380811055518158658620011249940702213853954895309218611738193261179310511854807446237996274956735188575272489122793818301194912"

# SHA-256-derived variable B(k) to reflect discrete e-digit windows for energy-driven modulation
def e_branch_factors(k, n_branches=4):
    """
    Variable B(k) from consecutive e digits window, normalized to [0.111, 1.0].
    """
    window = e_digits[k % len(e_digits):k % len(e_digits) + n_branches]
    while len(window) < n_branches:
        window += e_digits[:n_branches - len(window)]  # Wrap around
    vals = [int(d) for d in window]
    B = [v / 9 for v in vals]  # Normalize 1-9 to 0.111-1.0 (0 as gap)
    return B

# Simulation - Adjusting H, F, and dt for KRRB dynamics
H = np.pi / 9  # ≈ 0.349
F = 1.0  # Feedback factor
R0 = 1.0 + 0j  # Initial state
steps = 1000
dt = 0.5  # Time step

# Execute KRRB dynamics with variable B
R = krrb(R0, H, F, e_branch_factors, steps=steps, dt=dt)

# Plot magnitude and phase of the system
t = np.arange(steps + 1)
fig, (ax1, ax2) = plt.subplots(2, 1, figsize=(10, 8), sharex=True)
ax1.plot(t, np.abs(R), label="|R(t)|")
ax1.set_yscale('log')
ax1.set_ylabel("Magnitude")
ax1.legend()
ax1.grid(True)

ax2.plot(t, np.angle(R), label="arg(R(t))")
ax2.set_ylabel("Phase (rad)")
ax2.set_xlabel("Steps")
ax2.legend()
ax2.grid(True)

plt.suptitle("KRRB Execution with Variable e-Digit Branching")
plt.tight_layout()
plt.show()

# Now the KRRB evolution is plotted showing the magnitude and phase behavior over time.
```

This script simulates the dynamics using the KRRB evolution with variable branching factors derived from the digits of π. The plot visualizes both the magnitude and the phase of the recursive system.
